* [2025-1-13 Mon] - MSE of von Mises 
** consider replacing MSE for von Mises with MI replacing uniform distribution with von Mises distribution 
** explanation of MI and von Mises MSE 
Three possible scenarios:
1. High MI & low MSE: firing rate is strongly modulated by alpha phase 
2. Low MI & low MSE: firing rate is weakly modulated by alpha phase 
3. High MI & high MSE: disordered firing rate with respect to alpha phase. 
In the third case, the distribution of spikes across alpha phase differs strognly from both the uniform distribtion 
and the best fit von Mises distribtion (the circular analog to the normal distribution).
Since MI measures the distance from the uniform distribtion, and the von Mises distribtion 
can be equivalent to the uniform distribution if appropriately parameterized, there is no scenario in which MI is low and MSE
of the best fit von Mises distribution is high.

pfc = load('~/neuralctrl/projects/nCORTEx/Project_Selective-Attention/Experiments/SELECT_DETECT/Data/FTR/AP/FIG/PFC_Expert_Combo/PFC/Spontaneous_Alpha_Modulation_v2/data.mat');
pfc = pfc.out.alpha_modulated;
mses_von_mises = pfc.mses;
mses_bimodal = zeros(size(pfc,1),1);
for c = 1:size(pfc,1)
    [N, edges] = histcounts(pfc(c,:).spon_alpha_spike_phases{1}, 20, 'Normalization', 'pdf');
    binCenters = zeros(length(edges)-1,1);
    for e = 1:(length(edges)-1)
        binCenters(e) = mean(edges(e:(e+1)));
    end
    gmmModel = fitgmdist(pfc(c,:).spon_alpha_spike_phases{1}', 2);
    x = linspace(min(binCenters), max(binCenters), 1000); % Fine grid for PDF
    y = pdf(gmmModel, x'); % Evaluate GMM PDF
    y_interpolated = interp1(x, y, binCenters(2:end-1), 'linear');
    mses_bimodal(c) = mean((N(2:end-1) - y_interpolated').^2);
end

inds = find(mses_von_mises >  0.001 & mses_bimodal < 0.001);
for i = 1:length(inds)
    c = inds(i);
    [N, edges] = histcounts(pfc(c,:).spon_alpha_spike_phases{1}, 20, 'Normalization', 'pdf');
    binCenters = zeros(length(edges)-1,1);
    for e = 1:(length(edges)-1)
        binCenters(e) = mean(edges(e:(e+1)));
    end
    figure(); bar(binCenters, N, 'EdgeColor', 'k', 'FaceColor', [0.5,0.5,0.5], 'BarWidth', 1)
    ttl = strcat(pfc(c,:).session_id{1}, '_', num2str(pfc(c,:).cluster_id));
    title(ttl)
end

* [2025-1-31 Fri] - Discussion 
** overview of results 
*** physiology of alpha phase modulation of single neuron spiking activity in cortical and subcortical structures
*** relationship between baseline 
** other phasic relationships between spiking and lfp oscillations 
*** SPEAR model Hasselmo2025-xs 
separate phases of hippocampal theta for encoding and retrieval of memory 
*** phase precession in 
**** hippocampus: OKeefe1993-xh
**** entorhinal cortex: Hafting2008-vd
*** phasic relationships between noradrenergic neurons and PFC oscillations: Eschenko2012-wo
** should engage with Sobolewski2011-om 
Results: We observed a mean effect of desynchronization of the 5-12 Hz rhythm by the weak tactile stimulation. Arousal reduced the incidence of the 5-12 Hz oscillations and increased the desynchronizing
power of tactile stimuli.
Conclusions: Oscillations that can be disrupted by weak, purely tactile stimulation, and whose incidence
is reduced by increased arousal, should be interpreted as a physiological phenomenon typical for behavioral idling while the cerebral cortex maintains sensory sensitivity.
Significance: Our results contradict the view that the 5-12 Hz oscillatory activity, often observed in fronto-parietal cortical regions of Wistar rats, represents epileptic discharges. 
Rather, this activity provides a model for studying the physiology of alpha/mu oscillations.
** from Kelley2023-ir
*** origins of LFPs 
Rhythmic activity is ubiquitous in cortical structures of
the brain and can be measured as oscillations in the local
field potentials (LFPs) recorded extracellularly. LFPs are
largely generated by transmembrane currents, principally
postsynaptic dendritic currents, in neurons near the recording electrode (Buzsaki2012-ga, Engel2001-ap)
*** phase precession 
CNS phase coding has been most thoroughly studied in the context of “phase precession” in hippocampal place cells but has also been seen in entorhinal grid
cells (53), as well as in successive theta waves in medial prefrontal cortex (88), and ventral striatum (89). In hippocampal
place cells, the firing rate provides a coarse-grained encoding
of the animal's location in an arena (21). During phase precession, spiking occurs earlier on successive cycles of the
theta wave as an animal moves towards the center of a place
field, providing a finer-grained temporal encoding of location within the place field (54). 
** referencing Kelley2023-ir
While the resonant properties of some cortical neurons (namely layer 5b pyramidal neurons) promote phasic relationships with the 
LFP that change over time, most neurons lack these resonant properties, and simulations suggest they are more likely to maintain 
a single preffered phase of the LFP as seen in most of the phase modulated neurons we have recorded (following a von Mises distribution).
** comparisons to previous work 
*** Bollimunta2008-qh 
**** high coherence between multi-unit activity and LFP (CSD) in V1, V4, and IT in awake behaving monkeys 
**** low coherence between LFP and MUA in superficial/supragranular layers 
*** Bollimunta2011-nj
**** reduced spike field coherence in alpha in deep layers during cued visual attention 
***** such as different task, cued vs. uncued. execution of selective-detection task requires 
"calm yet prepared" state consistent with an early idling state hypothesis of the role of alpha 
oscilaltions.
**** copied 
The relative phase between CSD and MUA was computed to evaluate the relationship between firing probability and the
temporal sink-source organization of the alpha generator. The phase spectrum was found to be continuous in the alpha range
with a mean (n 5) phase difference of 16.0  6.4° at layer 6, 167.0  7.2° at layer 4C, 170.0  5.4° at layer 3B/4A, and
163.0  12.4° at layer 1/2. The phase relationship between CSD and MUA at layers 4C, 3B/4A, and 1/2 suggests that the
increased action potential firing in G and SG layers coincide with alpha current sinks. At layer 6, however, the near 
inphase relationship indicates that the current source is accompanied by an increase in MUA. A likely explanation is that
the recorded MUA at layer 6 contained a strong contribution from the depolarization of presynaptic thalamocortical axons
(Tenke et al., 1993; Schroeder et al., 1998; Swadlow et al., 2002).
*** Haegens2011-vh
**** Furthermore, we showed that decreasing alpha-power was associated with an increase in firing rate. *we did not*
**** Figure 2 shows all spikes and their relation to alpha phase
**** the pulsed-inhibition hypothesis (5, 28, 29), which states that the α-oscillations are
a consequence of bouts of inhibition that prevent firing and thus reduce neuronal processing.
***** 5 - Klimesch W, Sauseng P, Hanslmayr S (2007) EEG alpha oscillations: The inhibitiontiming hypothesis. Brain Res Brain Res Rev 53(1):63-88. 
***** 28 - Mazaheri A, Jensen O (2010) Rhythmic pulsing: Linking ongoing brain activity with evoked responses. Front Hum Neurosci 4:177.
***** 29 -  Mathewson KE, et al. (2011) Pulsed out of awareness: EEG alpha oscillations represent a pulsed-inhibition of ongoing cortical processing. Front Psychol 2:99.
*** Haegens2015-mx 
**** again using CSD-MUA coherence 
*** Dougherty2017-ek 
ongoing infragranular alpha-range fluctuations in V1 play a role in regulating columnar visual activity
*** Examining the phasic relationship between oscillations and MUA can be misleading; although the network may spike most 
often at the trough of the alpha oscillation, a number of neurons instead fir near the peak of alpha, or spike according
to a bimodal distribution with respect to alpha phase.
*** Lakatos2005-hp 
**** evaluates phasic relationships between delta, theta, and gamma and MUA in auditory cortex of 
****  This oscillatory hierarchy controls baseline excitability and thus stimulus-related responses 
in a neuronal ensemble. We propose that the hierarchical organization of ambient oscillatory 
activity allows auditory cortex to structure its temporal activity pattern so as to optimize the 
processing of rhythmic inputs.
*** Davis2023-rd
**** phasic relationships between evoked broad band LFPs and MUA across cortical layers 
**** "laminar CSD analysis requires averaging across reliably evoked responses that target the input layer in cortex"
**** PFC and MT in marmoset, V4 in macacque 
**** shows modulation strength and preferred angle decreased with cortical depth 
*** Golden2022-bt
**** Psilocybin reduces low frequency oscillatory power and neuronal phase-locking in the anterior cingulate cortex of awake rodents
**** multi-unit activity and its relationship with to LFP phase across bands, except gamma 
*** Lazaro2019-cp
**** reduction of phase locking of single unit spiking activity to slow LFP oscillations in mPFC in autism mouse model compared to WT control 
*** Hyman2011-pw
**** how mpfc neurons are phase locked to hippocampal theta phase and its functional relevance 
*** Mazaheri2010-td
We postulate that the amplitude asymmetric alpha activity
can be viewed as rhythmic pulses producing bouts of inhibition
repeated every 100 ms. How might this inhibition come about?
GABAergic feedbacks from interneurons have been strongly implicated in the physiological mechanism generating the alpha rhythm
(Lopes da Silva et al., 1976; Crunelli and Leresche, 1991; Jones
et al., 2000). Thus it is possible that rhythmic neuronal activity
generating the alpha oscillations is a consequence of a GABAergic
inhibitory feedback paced by neocortical or thalamic rhythm generators (Hughes and Crunelli, 2005; Lorincz et al., 2008, 2009). 
This GABAergic feedback could serve to directly silence processing in pyramidal neurons or reduce the efficacy of excitatory input by
shunting inhibition.
*** Buffalo2011-cp
**** reduced spike field coherence in alpha in deep layers during cued visual attention 
***** such as different task, cued vs. uncued. execution of selective-detection task requires 
"calm yet prepared" state consistent with an early idling state hypothesis of the role of alpha 
oscilaltions.

init_paths;
sessionIDs;
% session_id = expert_3387_session_ids{1};
counts_high = zeros(length(expert_3387_session_ids),5);
counts_low = zeros(length(expert_3387_session_ids),5);
for s = 1:length(expert_3387_session_ids)
    session_id = expert_3387_session_ids{s};
    load(sprintf('%sAP/%s.mat', ext_path, session_id));
    load(sprintf('%sLFP/%s.mat', ext_path, session_id));
    load(sprintf('%sSLRT/%s.mat', ext_path, session_id));
    all_powers = [];
    all_times = [];
    for t = 1:size(lfp_data,1)
        t_power = [];
        t_time = [];
        for c = 1:size(lfp_data(t,:).lfp{1},1)
            lfp = lfp_data(t,:).lfp{1}(c,:);
            if strcmp(slrt_data(t,:).categorical_outcome{1}, 'Hit') || strcmp(slrt_data(t,:).categorical_outcome{1}, 'Miss')
                lfp_times = lfp_data(t,:).left_trigger_aligned_lfp_time{1};
                lfp_time = lfp_data(t,:).lfpTime{1};
            else
                lfp_times = lfp_data(t,:).right_trigger_aligned_lfp_time{1};
                lfp_time = lfp_data(t,:).lfpTime{1};
            end
            alpha = bandpassFilter(lfp, 8, 12, 500);
            phi = angle(hilbert(alpha));
            ALPHA = abs(hilbert(alpha)).^2;
            delta = bandpassFilter(lfp, 1, 4, 500);
            DELTA = abs(hilbert(delta)).^2;
            t_power = [t_power; ALPHA(lfp_times > -3 & lfp_times < 0)];
            t_time = [t_time; lfp_time(lfp_times > -3 & lfp_times < 0)];
        end
        all_powers = [all_powers, t_power];
        all_times = [all_times, t_time];
    end
    high_outcomes = {};
    low_outcomes = {};
    high_sessions = {};
    low_sessions = {};
    high_phases = [];
    low_phases = [];
    high_frs = [];
    low_frs = [];
    all_high = {};
    all_low = {};
    for t = 1:size(lfp_data,1)
        t_high = {};
        t_low = {};
        for c = 1:size(all_powers,1)
            lfp = lfp_data(t,:).lfp{1}(c,:);
            if strcmp(slrt_data(t,:).categorical_outcome{1}, 'Hit') || strcmp(slrt_data(t,:).categorical_outcome{1}, 'Miss')
                lfp_times = lfp_data(t,:).left_trigger_aligned_lfp_time{1};
            else
                lfp_times = lfp_data(t,:).right_trigger_aligned_lfp_time{1};
            end
            alpha = bandpassFilter(lfp, 8, 12, 500);
            phi = angle(hilbert(alpha));
            ALPHA = abs(hilbert(alpha)).^2;
            delta = bandpassFilter(lfp, 1, 4, 500);
            DELTA = abs(hilbert(delta)).^2;
            ALPHA = ALPHA(lfp_times > -3 & lfp_times < 0);
            lfp_times = lfp_times(lfp_times > -3 & lfp_times < 0);
            high_inds = findEvents(ALPHA, lfp_times, prctile(all_powers(c,:), 75), 0.33, 0.2, 'above');
            low_inds = findEvents(ALPHA, lfp_times, prctile(all_powers(c,:), 50), 0.33, 0.2, 'below');
            t_high{c} = high_inds;
            t_low{c} = low_inds;
        end
        all_high{t} = t_high;
        all_low{t} = t_low;
    end
    outcomes = {'Hit', 'Miss', 'CR', 'FA', 'Pass'};

    c = 50;
    for t = 1:size(slrt_data)
        ind = find(strcmp(outcomes, slrt_data(t,:).categorical_outcome{1}));
        counts_high(s,ind) = counts_high(ind) + size(all_high{t}{c},2);
        counts_low(s,ind) = counts_low(ind) + size(all_low{t}{c},2);
    end
end

Reviewer: 1

Comments to Author
This study by Kelley et al. investigates the coupling of spiking activity to the phase of alpha oscillations in multiple brain regions in mice performing a 
whisker-based selective detection task. The strength of this study is the ability to relate spike-phase coupling to task performance. While there have been 
multiple theories proposed as to the functions of alpha oscillations, few studies have been able to directly test these theories with spike-phase resolution 
in subjects performing goal-directed tasks. That said, there are multiple problems with the manuscript in its current form.

Major concerns

1. Only 'Go' sided recordings and analyses. While the datasets appear to be high quality, all four probe implants were contralateral to the Go stimulus. 
This precludes the ability to compare 'Go' vs 'NoGo' cortical and subcortical dynamics. As motivated in the Introduction, differences in alpha power 
have been associated with 'attended' vs 'unattended' cortices, thereby potentially contributing to the suppression of irrelevant representations. By 
only sampling the 'Go' hemisphere, the authors are unable to test a major motivating hypothesis.

2. Main conclusion: alpha modulation is lower on incorrect trials. If supported by the data, this would be an important finding. However, my concern is about 
the statistical analyses used to support this conclusion. The conclusion appears to be driven by the data presented in Figure 6 (example data) and 7A (summary data). 
The problem is with the number of trials included in each analysis. There are many more 'hit' and 'correct rejection' trials in expert mice, providing more statistical 
power than for 'miss' and 'false alarm' trials, which may be driving the results in Figure 7A. Indeed, the MI in 7B is higher for misses for most datasets. On p14, 
line 26 the authors state “While higher MI may be mistaken for stronger coupling, higher MI combined with the magnitude of MSE observed on incorrect trials (compare 
to MSE in Figs. 3-5) is better explained by an increased randomness in the relationship between spiking activity and alpha phase.” This may be true if the same number 
of spikes are included in both analyses. My suggestion - re-do these analyses with similar spike numbers per trial type. This would greatly strengthen the robustness 
of the findings. More generally, the dependence of spike rates / spike counts on phase modulation statistical analyses should be considered throughout the study.

*** DONE: consider find neurons with high spike counts on incorrect trials 
**** don't think that's way to go 
*** DONE: maybe pick as many hits/correct rejection trials as there are miss/false alarm trials at random and generate distribution
***** AlphaModFigScripts/correctIncorrectShuffle.m

3. Interpretation of alpha modulation. The messaging here is unclear. The authors write on p17 “The strong alpha modulation observed prior to correct trials compared 
to incorrect trials, rather than reflecting the sort of internally directed attention described in Buffalo et al. (2011), instead may produce the global low amplitude 
activity, identified by Marrero et al. (2022), which tunes brain networks to the discrete all-or-none behavior required for this task (Hô and Destexhe, 2000).” 
However, this seems to contradict their own data showing a lack of difference in baseline firing rates prior to hit vs miss trials. Moreover, the authors propose 
“this global cortical low amplitude state is, at least in part, a result of the ubiquity of alpha modulation and pulsed inhibition in neocortex.” And yet, from their 
own data, alpha modulation levels did not influence spike rates. These inconsistencies need to be reconciled.

alpha modulation and trial outcomes? If face/body movements were monitored during these experiments, their analyses relative to alpha modulations and trial outcomes 
should be included.

5. Interpretation of alpha modulation, part 2. Throughout the manuscript the authors suggest that (from the abstract) “dysregulation of spiking activity with respect 
to alpha oscillations may characterize lapses in task engagement or attention.” However, if low alpha modulation reflected poor task engagement, one would expect 
reduced responding (including false alarms), rather than reduced correct outcomes. Impairments in attention, however, would be consistent with reduced correct outcomes.

Figure labels on p10 and 11 referencing figure 3 appear to be mislabeled.

Results, general. Since alpha power correlates with spike-phase coupling strength, the authors could confirm their results by quantifying alpha power prior to different 
trial outcomes.


Reviewer: 2

Comments to Author
The manuscript by Kelley and colleagues presents a well-motivated and technically ambitious study that studies the relationship between alpha activity (phase and power) 
and spiking dynamics in different brain areas of mice. The task is clever, requiring selective responses to a particular stimulation on the body, thus probing dynamics 
during task-dependent states. The project integrates advanced signal processing techniques and large-datasets collected using high-density Neuropixels probes. The 
introduction is well-written and provides a strong conceptual foundation for the work. Particularly commendable is the authors' application of a 1/f correction in 
their oscillatory analyses, which demonstrates thoughtful attention to the spectral characteristics of neural signals. However, there are many issues with the methods, 
analyses, and interpretations that substantially dampen my enthusiasm for the paper. The current version of the manuscript requires substantial revision to address concerns 
related to statistical interpretation, methodological clarity, and data presentation. However, with thoughtful revisions, this study has the potential to make a significant 
contribution to our understanding of oscillatory phase-spike relationships in the behaving brain. Overall, the potential findings may be of broad interest to the readership 
associated with Cerebral Cortex. Below I provide a point-by-point description of my major and minor concerns.

Major concerns

1. Are the data pooled data across entire sessions? If so, this raises a significant concern. Behavioral performance in mice often fluctuates within a session—typically 
improving after the initial trials, stabilizing mid-session, and then declining towards the end of the block due to fatigue or reduced motivation. It is unclear whether 
the authors accounted for these within-session dynamics in their analyses. Did they segment the sessions to examine performance trends over time, or verify that performance 
remained stable throughout? Without such verification, pooling data across the entire session could introduce confounds that affect the interpretation of both behavioral and 
neural results. The authors should provide evidence that performance was consistent across the session or reanalyze the data in a time-resolved manner to ensure the robustness 
of their findings.

This concern raises specific concerns for the alpha phase/spiking effects. Indeed, it is well-established that alpha-band power tends to increase during states of reduced arousal, 
such as eye-closed conditions, mind wandering, or low motivation. This known relationship raises concerns about the stability and interpretation of the reported alpha-phase modulation 
effects across the duration of experimental sessions. In particular, if high alpha power is disproportionately concentrated toward the latter portions of the sessions—a plausible 
scenario—it becomes challenging to determine whether the observed modulations of spiking activity reflect task-specific neural mechanisms or are instead driven by global changes 
in arousal or vigilance. To address this issue, the authors should quantify and report the temporal distribution of high-alpha epochs throughout the session.
** TODOS

*** TODO: covariance of alpha modulation with binned d', hr, far 
*** TODO: distribution of alpha events during the session 

2. While Kilosort is a widely used and powerful tool for automated spike sorting, prior studies have noted limitations in its ability to accurately isolate single units under certain 
conditions (Pachitariu et al Nature Methods 2024). To improve the reliability of spike classification, it is common practice to apply additional post hoc validation or refinement 
procedures. It is unclear whether the authors implemented any such follow-up analyses to verify the quality of their spike sorting results. For example, did they assess waveform 
consistency using PCA, examine the spatial distance separation of recorded units, analyze correlations in spike timing to detect potential sorting errors, or other metrics? 
Clarification on these points is important to ensure the validity of the reported single-unit data, especially as they relate to the classification of fast-spiking and regular-spiking 
cells.

3. While the results presented in Figure 4 are potentially interesting, it is difficult to assess whether the interpretations are fully supported by the analyses. Several methodological 
and reporting issues limit the clarity and validity of the conclusions drawn from these data.

o Statistical Concerns: The statistical tests used to evaluate differences in proportions across conditions appear to be inappropriate. For instance, in Figures 3B and 4A (top panel), 
the authors assess binary outcomes (e.g., whether a unit was modulated or not) using parametric tests such as ANOVAs, and in other cases, non-parametric tests such as Wilcoxon 
signed-rank tests. However, these tests assume continuous or ordinal data, not proportions derived from binary classifications. Instead, tests designed for categorical data 
(e.g., Chi-square tests) would be more appropriate. The authors should revise these analyses accordingly or provide a clear justification for the statistical approach used.

o Interpretation of Firing Rate Changes: The changes in firing rate presented in Figures 4B and 4D are not clearly contextualized. It is unclear what these changes are relative 
to—baseline, another condition, or a specific event. This lack of clarity undermines the interpretability of these results. The authors should explicitly define the reference 
period or condition used in these comparisons.

computations and statistical methods used are necessary to allow proper evaluation of the results.

o Identification of Anatomical Structures: It is unclear whether the recordings span the full depth of a cortical column, particularly for the S1 recordings. Confirmation that 
recordings were obtained across cortical layers is essential for interpreting laminar differences in neural activity. While the use of Neuropixels Trajectory Explorer provides a 
useful estimate, it is not sufficient on its own to verify precise laminar localization. The authors should provide additional empirical evidence—such as current source density 
(CSD) analyses—to support their claim that recordings traverse the cortical column.  Moreover, for PFC recordings, how did the authors validate that the recordings were obtained 
from the alleged regions (e.g., ACC, PL, IL, ORB, DP).

4. The rationale for selectively presenting only a subset of the data in Figure 6 is unclear. Specifically, the figure includes only fast-spiking (FS) neurons from S1 and 
regular-spiking (RS) neurons from the prefrontal cortex, without explanation for the omission of other cell types or regions. The authors should clarify their criteria for 
inclusion and justify why only these particular subsets are shown. If data from additional neuronal populations or regions were analyzed, those results should either be included 
or the rationale for their exclusion explicitly stated.

5. While the introduction is clearly written, the remainder of the manuscript would benefit from substantial editorial refinement. One major concern is the considerable redundancy 
throughout the Results section. In several instances, the authors reintroduce methodological details that have already been described in the Methods section. This repetition 
detracts from the flow and conciseness of the manuscript. I recommend that the authors streamline the Results section by removing or substantially abbreviating procedural 
descriptions that are already covered elsewhere. As a specific example, lines 34-43 on page 7 reiterate content that is already thoroughly explained in the Methods and can 
be omitted without loss of clarity. Additionally, for the population-level analyses, it would be helpful to include a summary table indicating the proportion of fast-spiking (FS) 
versus regular-spiking (RS) units recorded in each brain region. This would enhance interpretability and allow for clearer cross-area comparisons. There are a lot fo data 
presented. Perhaps, it would be better to just focus on a narrower set of results. Finally, the Discussion section is overly long and should be condensed. This is particularly 
important if any re-analysis of the data leads to results that conflict with the current interpretations. In such cases, the narrative should be revised to accurately reflect the 
revised findings while maintaining clarity and focus.

6. The rationale for classifying neurons based on extracellular waveform shape requires further clarification and justification. While this approach has been historically used to 
distinguish putative inhibitory (e.g., parvalbumin-expressing, fast-spiking neurons) from excitatory (regular-spiking) cell types, the waveform-based classification is limited 
in reliability. This method is sensitive to technical factors such as electrode impedance, recording depth, and the local cellular milieu, which can significantly distort waveform 
features. Additionally, unsupervised clustering approaches (e.g., k-means) applied to waveform features are highly dataset-dependent (i.e., dependent on the distribution of 
the waveshape), and the resulting classifications may not generalize across sessions or animals. Prior studies (e.g., Shin and Moore, Neuron, 2019) have attempted to mitigate 
these limitations by restricting analyses to neurons with waveform properties at the extreme ends of the distribution, thereby reducing classification ambiguity. However, it 
appears that such thresholding was not implemented in the current study. Given that the work is performed in mice, why not consider leveraging molecular biology tools 
(e.g., optogenetics) to more definitively classify cell types, which would substantially strengthen the interpretability of their findings.
** TODOS:

*** TODO: for RS/FS comparisons, consider sampling from extremes of the distributions 

7. The statistical analyses appear to be conducted primarily at the population level (i.e., across cells), but it is unclear whether significance was also evaluated at 
the level of individual units. This distinction is critical, as population-level significance can emerge even when individual cells do not show robust effects, and vice versa. 
Such discrepancies could lead to different interpretations of the findings. The authors should clarify whether individual cell-level statistical tests were performed and, if so, 
report the proportion of cells meeting significance criteria. Conversely, in cases where no significant population-level effects were observed, it would be informative to assess 
whether any subpopulation of cells exhibited consistent modulation.
** TODOS:
*** TODO: clarify cell vs. population level analyses 

8. The authors state in the Statistics section that they employed “circular ANOVAs.” However, there is no evidence of these analyses in the main text or Methods. 
It would be helpful for the authors to clarify whether they indeed used circular ANOVA or if this was a reference to an alternative test (e.g., the Watson-Williams 
test or a test based on the von Mises distribution). Please revise the text to specify the exact statistical method used and ensure consistency between the methods 
section and the analyses described in the results.
** actual line: "Watson-Williams test was used as a circular analog of ANOVA (Berens 2009)"

9. The criterion for determining significant phase modulation—namely, applying a Bonferroni correction by dividing the Rayleigh test p-value threshold (0.05) by 
the number of units recorded in a given session—appears to be highly conservative. While controlling for multiple comparisons is important, such a stringent 
correction may increase the risk of Type II errors, potentially masking less-strong phase-locked effects. The authors should justify their choice of correction method 
or reanalyze the data to provide a more balanced approach.
** from chatgpt -- Some commonly used methods include:
*** Holm-Bonferroni method: A stepwise version of Bonferroni that is uniformly more powerful. It sequentially adjusts the p-values and 
stops testing once a non-significant result is encountered.
*** Benjamini-Hochberg procedure (FDR control): Controls the false discovery rate rather than the family-wise error rate. This is 
particularly useful when you expect some true positives among many tests (e.g., in genomics or neuroimaging).
*** Hochberg's method: Similar to Holm's, but slightly more powerful; it requires independence or positive dependence among tests.
*** False Discovery Rate (FDR) variants, such as Benjamini-Yekutieli, which accommodate arbitrary dependence structures.
Each method has trade-offs between controlling Type I errors and retaining power. The choice depends on the context and the acceptable level of false positives.

10. The interpretation of the results presented in Figures 3D-G requires further clarification. As currently stated, the authors appear to argue that a higher mean 
squared error (MSE) from the von Mises fit indicates a deviation from a unimodal circular distribution, thereby reflecting a less phase-aligned neural response. While this 
interpretation is plausible, it overlooks the possibility that a high MSE could also arise from a structured but non-unimodal distribution—such as a bimodal (e.g., two-peaked) 
von Mises distribution, which appears to be the case in Figure 3C. In such scenarios, the elevated MSE would not necessarily reflect increased noise or reduced phase locking, 
but rather a meaningful shift in the shape of the distribution. The authors should clarify how they distinguish between these possibilities and consider alternative model fits 
(e.g., mixture models) if appropriate.

11. In Figure 2H, what are the units on the x-axis? If it's time (e.g., seconds), then it is odd that the oscillatory phase remains so consistent? Please clarify.

in radians, no? Please clarify.

13. The authors repeatedly assert that neural activity increases in response to reward delivery (e.g., line 30 on page 10). However, the neural data appear to be 
time-locked to stimulus onset rather than to the reward itself. This raises concerns about the validity of the claim that the observed activity is specifically 
related to reward processing. Without aligning neural responses to the time of reward delivery, it is difficult to disentangle whether the reported effects reflect 
responses to the stimulus, motor actions, reward anticipation, or the reward itself. The authors should either revise their interpretation accordingly or provide analyses 
aligned to the actual time of reward delivery to substantiate these claims.
** TODOS:
*** DONE: add dashed lines indicating reward time (where necessary)

14. It is commendable that the animals were trained on a selective detection task; however, it remains unclear whether the rewarded side was fixed or alternated across sessions. 
This detail is essential for interpreting whether the observed alpha-band modulations are associated with attention to the task-relevant target or with suppression of the distractor. 
Without this information, it is difficult to disentangle whether the neural signatures reflect enhancement of relevant input or suppression of irrelevant input. There are studies 
that have raised concerns whether mechanisms of distracter suppression (e.g., the alpha suppression effect, see Snyder and Foxe, Frontiers in Psychology, 2011) are independent of 
target enhancement. The findings from this paper can shed light onto this discussion by clarifying the reward structure and its implications for interpreting alpha dynamics.
** TODOS:
*** DONE: clarify that rewarded side was fixed across sessions 

15. The rationale for including more than twice as many trials in the no-go stimulus condition compared to the go condition is unclear. This imbalance in trial numbers could 
introduce biases the responses of animals, and thus the findings on the neural data. The authors should clarify the reasoning behind this design choice and discuss whether 
the unequal trial distribution may have influenced their results or statistical comparisons.

On a related note, it seems that mice with higher rates also had higher false alarms. This would suggest a response bias in the dataset (i.e., mice were just more prone to 
lick in general). The authors should address this, and investigate whether it can influence the current findings.

    inds = find(strcmp(slrt_data.categorical_outcome, 'Miss'));
    for i = 1:length(inds)
        ind = inds(i);
        all_phases = [];

        end
        p_miss(i) = circ_rtest(all_phases);
    end
    inds = find(strcmp(slrt_data.categorical_outcome, 'FA'));
    for i = 1:length(inds)
        ind = inds(i);
        all_phases = [];
        for c = 1:length(cids)
            phases = ap_data(ind,:).spiking_data{1}(ap_data(ind,:).spiking_data{1}.cluster_id == cids(c),:).spike_alpha_phase{1};
            all_phases = [all_phases, phases(ap_data(ind,:).spiking_data{1}(ap_data(ind,:).spiking_data{1}.cluster_id == cids(c),:).right_trigger_aligned_spike_times{1} < 0)]; 
        end
        if ~isempty(all_phases)
            p_fa(i) = circ_rtest(all_phases);
        else
            p_fa(i) = nan;
        end
    end
    inds = find(strcmp(slrt_data.categorical_outcome, 'CR'));

        ind = inds(i);  
        all_phases = [];

            all_phases = [all_phases, phases(ap_data(ind,:).spiking_data{1}(ap_data(ind,:).spiking_data{1}.cluster_id == cids(c),:).right_trigger_aligned_spike_times{1} < 0)]; 
        end
        p_cr(i) = circ_rtest(all_phases);
    end
end

fig = figure('Position', [1243 1123 1601 548]);
subplot(1,2,1)
hold on 
s1_rs_all_lick_frs = cellfun(@nanmean, out.s1_rs_all_lick_frs);
s1_fs_all_lick_frs = cellfun(@nanmean, out.s1_fs_all_lick_frs);
pfc_rs_all_lick_frs = cellfun(@nanmean, out.pfc_rs_all_lick_frs);
pfc_fs_all_lick_frs = cellfun(@nanmean, out.pfc_fs_all_lick_frs);

striatum_fs_all_lick_frs = cellfun(@nanmean, out.striatum_fs_all_lick_frs);

s1_fs_all_no_lick_frs = cellfun(@nanmean, out.s1_fs_all_no_lick_frs);
pfc_rs_all_no_lick_frs = cellfun(@nanmean, out.pfc_rs_all_no_lick_frs);
pfc_fs_all_no_lick_frs = cellfun(@nanmean, out.pfc_fs_all_no_lick_frs);
striatum_rs_all_no_lick_frs = cellfun(@nanmean, out.striatum_rs_all_no_lick_frs);
striatum_fs_all_no_lick_frs = cellfun(@nanmean, out.striatum_fs_all_no_lick_frs);
for i = 1:length(s1_rs_all_lick_frs)
    x = (rand()-0.5) * 0.5;
    plot(1+x, s1_rs_all_no_lick_frs(i), 'r.')
    plot(2+x, s1_rs_all_lick_frs(i), 'b.')
    plot([1,2]+x, [s1_rs_all_no_lick_frs(i), s1_rs_all_lick_frs(i)], '--', 'Color', [0.5, 0.5, 0.5], 'LineWidth', 0.1)
end
for i = 1:length(s1_fs_all_lick_frs)

    plot(4+x, s1_fs_all_no_lick_frs(i), 'r.')

end
for i = 1:length(pfc_rs_all_lick_frs)

end

    plot(10+x, pfc_fs_all_no_lick_frs(i), 'r.')

end
for i = 1:length(striatum_rs_all_lick_frs)

    plot(13+x, striatum_rs_all_no_lick_frs(i), 'r.')

end

end
errorbar([2,5,8,11,14,17], ...
    [nanmean(cellfun(@nanmean, out.s1_rs_all_lick_frs)), nanmean(cellfun(@nanmean, out.s1_fs_all_lick_frs)), ...
    nanmean(cellfun(@nanmean, out.pfc_rs_all_lick_frs)), nanmean(cellfun(@nanmean, out.pfc_fs_all_lick_frs)), ...
